On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"155dbbc2-8ddf-4b80-b5f2-0358aad24254","session_id":"8abe6239-b961-4eaf-a88d-781fc0f90061","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_5f27119e03644ac785546044","is_error":false,"content":"# User account registration and authentication (JDBC)\n\n`UserRegistration.java` creates user accounts in a relational database and lets\nthose stored credentials authenticate a later login. It works on a JDBC\n`Connection` the caller already opened and owns.\n\n* Java 17, no dependencies beyond the JDK for the library itself.\n* One source file, no build tool required.\n\n---\n\n## Password handling\n\nThe password is never stored, never returned by any method, and never\nconcatenated into SQL. What reaches the database is a salted, stretched digest:\n\n```\npbkdf2-sha256$600000$Base64(16-byte salt)$Base64(32-byte digest)\n```\n\n| Decision | Why |\n| --- | --- |\n| **PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 600,000 iterations** | Stretches each guess so an offline crack of a stolen table is expensive. 600,000 is OWASP's 2023 floor for this primitive; measured at **860 ms** per derivation on one core of this machine. |\n| **Fresh 16-byte salt per account** (`SecureRandom`) | Two users with the same password get different rows, so one precomputed rainbow table cannot cover the table and identical rows do not leak who shares a secret. |\n| **Algorithm and iteration count stored in the row** | Verification uses the cost that was in force when the account was created, so raising the default later logs nobody out and invalidates nothing. |\n| **Stored-cost bounds (1,000 - 10,000,000)** | A tampered or corrupt row cannot ask for 1 round (instant cracking) or 10^9 rounds (login denial of service). |\n| **`MessageDigest.isEqual` for the digest compare** | Byte comparison that does not return early on the first differing byte. |\n| **Junk input fails closed** | A malformed, foreign-format, or unsupported stored digest yields \"does not match\", never an exception that reaches a login page and never an accidental match. |\n| **`char[]` in, `clear()` available** | Callers can wipe the secret instead of parking it in an immutable `String`. The library never modifies the caller's array and zeroes its own scratch (`PBEKeySpec`, derived key bytes) as soon as it is done. |\n| **Passwords the JCE would fold together are refused** | `PBEKeySpec` is not injective over `char[]`: it drops trailing NULs and maps every unpaired surrogate to a single `?`, which makes distinct passwords derive the same digest. See *Why some passwords are refused*. |\n| **Unknown usernames still pay for a derivation** | The lookup-miss path runs PBKDF2 against a throwaway digest, so response time cannot be used to enumerate which usernames have accounts. Measured unknown-user / wrong-password latency ratio: **1.00-1.02** across runs. |\n\n`String` is the wrong type for a password in Java: it is interned, may be\ncached, and cannot be overwritten. `char[]` is used for that reason. Zeroing it\nis still only best effort -- it narrows the window in which the secret is\nreachable in heap, it cannot retract a copy the JVM or your own code already\nmade, and it does nothing about swap.\n\n### Why some passwords are refused\n\n`PBEKeySpec` converts a `char[]` password to bytes with the JCE's own UTF-8\nconverter, and that converter is **not injective across the whole `char[]`\ndomain**. Both behaviours below were measured directly on this JVM\n(OpenJDK 17.0.20.1), deriving with the raw JCE:\n\n| Password (chars) | PBKDF2 digest |\n| --- | --- |\n| `c a f é` | `4a93db32…f220f9d8` |\n| `c a f é` + NUL | `4a93db32…f220f9d8` |\n| `c a f é` + NUL + NUL | `4a93db32…f220f9d8` |\n| `q` + U+D800 | `460aaca2…4b1a7b5a` |\n| `q` + U+D801 | `460aaca2…4b1a7b5a` |\n| `q` + U+DC00 | `460aaca2…4b1a7b5a` |\n| `q` + `?` | `460aaca2…4b1a7b5a` |\n\nEach group is a set of *different* passwords that produce *the same* digest, so\na user who registered one could be logged in by anyone who presented another.\nThat is precisely the failure a password digest exists to prevent, and it is not\nsomething a hashing wrapper notices on its own.\n\n`UserRegistration` therefore refuses both classes in `hash` and in `matches`:\n\n* a password ending in NUL;\n* a password containing an unpaired surrogate.\n\nRefusing rather than \"cleaning up\" is deliberate: trimming or replacing\ncharacters would silently change which secret the user typed. Correctly paired\nsupplementary characters (emoji, astral scripts) are unambiguous and remain\nvalid; an *interior* NUL is unambiguous too (only trailing ones are discarded)\nand is also accepted. The test suite asserts both halves -- that each collapse\nreally occurs at the raw JCE, that the guard rejects every member of each class,\nand that legitimate Unicode still round-trips -- so the guard cannot be deleted\nor half-deleted without a check failing.\n\nOne consequence worth stating because it is easy to get wrong: since a refused\ncandidate never reaches the KDF, `authenticate` charges it the guard digest's\nderivation anyway. A free refusal would be a username oracle -- present `q` +\nU+D800 and a fast answer means the row exists. The suite measures that path too\n(refused-candidate to wrong-password ratio **0.99-1.04**).\n\n\n## SQL handling\n\n* Every statement that carries user-supplied data is a `PreparedStatement` with\n  `?` placeholders. A username such as `admin' OR '1'='1` is data, never\n  statement text. The only thing concatenated into SQL is the table name, a\n  compile-time constant.\n* The lookup compares with `username = ?`. Not `LIKE` -- under `LIKE`, `_` is a\n  single-character wildcard, so `alicex1` would satisfy a lookup for `alice_1`\n  and hand out an account name to guess against.\n* Usernames are matched case-sensitively and verbatim. No `UPPER()` folding\n  (`alice` must not be able to log in as `Alice`) and no trimming.\n* Usernames are validated against the allowlist `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{3,64}` before\n  any statement is prepared. Passwords must be non-empty and no longer than\n  1,024 characters, because PBKDF2 cost is linear in password length and an\n  unbounded field lets anyone make registration arbitrarily expensive.\n* Uniqueness is enforced by a `UNIQUE` index, not by a check-then-insert race.\n  The database is the authority; the loser of a concurrent signup gets\n  `USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` and the existing row is left untouched.\n* `Statement`, `ResultSet` and `PreparedStatement` are closed with\n  try-with-resources. The caller's `Connection` is never closed.\n\n## Schema\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS app_users (\n    id            BIGINT GENERATED BY DEFAULT AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,\n    username      VARCHAR(64)  NOT NULL,\n    password_hash VARCHAR(200) NOT NULL,\n    created_at    TIMESTAMP    NOT NULL\n);\nCREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS ux_app_users_username ON app_users (username);\n```\n\n`UserRegistry.ensureSchema()` runs exactly this DDL, so the table does not have\nto pre-exist. A digest is ~90 characters, which leaves room in `VARCHAR(200)`\nfor longer iteration counts. The DDL is portable across H2, PostgreSQL, MariaDB\nand MySQL 8; on Oracle/SQL Server adjust the identity and timestamp clauses.\n\n## API\n\n```java\npublic final class UserRegistration {\n    public enum RegistrationOutcome   { CREATED, USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS }\n    public enum AuthenticationOutcome { AUTHENTICATED, NO_SUCH_USER, BAD_PASSWORD }\n\n    public static final class PasswordHasher {          // thread-safe\n        public PasswordHasher();                        // 600,000 iterations\n        public PasswordHasher(int iterations);\n        public String  hash(char[] password);           // the value to store\n        public boolean matches(char[] password, String storedDigest);\n        public static void clear(char[] password);      // best-effort wipe\n        public static boolean isVerifiableCandidate(char[] password);\n    }\n\n    public static final class UserRegistry {            // one per Connection\n        public UserRegistry(Connection connection, PasswordHasher hasher);\n        public UserRegistry(Connection connection, PasswordHasher hasher, boolean commitOwnWork);\n        public void ensureSchema() throws SQLException;\n        public RegistrationOutcome   register(String username, char[] password) throws SQLException;\n        public AuthenticationOutcome authenticate(String username, char[] password) throws SQLException;\n        public boolean exists(String username) throws SQLException;\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Typical use\n\n```java\n// One hasher for the whole application; it is thread-safe.\nUserRegistration.PasswordHasher hasher = new UserRegistration.PasswordHasher();\n\n// One registry per connection, i.e. typically one per request.\nUserRegistration.UserRegistry registry = new UserRegistration.UserRegistry(connection, hasher);\n\nchar[] password = readPasswordFromConsole();       // e.g. System.console().readPassword()\ntry {\n    UserRegistration.RegistrationOutcome outcome = registry.register(\"alice\", password);\n    if (outcome == UserRegistration.RegistrationOutcome.USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS) {\n        // ask for a different username\n    }\n} finally {\n    UserRegistration.PasswordHasher.clear(password);\n}\n```\n\n`IllegalArgumentException` means the input was rejected and nothing was written;\n`SQLException` means the database failed.\n\n### Login\n\n`AuthenticationOutcome.NO_SUCH_USER` and `BAD_PASSWORD` are kept distinct for\nserver-side logging and migration tooling. **Show the caller one identical\nmessage for both** (\"invalid username or password\"), log the distinction\nserver-side only, and put rate limiting and lockout in front of this call.\nDistinguishable wording or response sizes turn the timing equalisation above\nback into a username oracle.\n\n### Transactions\n\n`register` does not open or close a transaction on its own. If the connection\nhas manual commits and the registry was built with `commitOwnWork = true` (the\ndefault), a successful insert is committed and a failed one rolled back. Pass\n`false` when the surrounding unit of work decides when to commit; the rollback\nthen belongs to that unit of work too.\n\n---\n\n## Compile and run\n\nEverything below was run on OpenJDK 17.0.20.1 on this machine. Sources are\nASCII-only, so no `-encoding` flag is needed (the platform default charset here\nis US-ASCII).\n\n### Run the demonstration\n\nThe demo needs a JDBC driver; H2 is used here only so the example is runnable.\n\n```bash\njavac -d out UserRegistration.java\njava -cp out:h2.jar UserRegistration\n```\n\nOptionally point it at your own database:\n`java -cp out:h2.jar UserRegistration <jdbc-url> <user> <password>`.\n\nActual output on this machine:\n\n```\nregister alice            -> CREATED\nregister alice (twice)    -> USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS\nregister bob              -> CREATED\nauthenticate alice/right  -> AUTHENTICATED\nauthenticate alice/wrong  -> BAD_PASSWORD\nauthenticate carol/right  -> NO_SUCH_USER\nregister quoted payload -> refused by validation: username must be 3-64 characters of [A-Za-z0-9._-], got: length 16\nregister admin--          -> CREATED\nauthenticate admin--/right-> AUTHENTICATED\nauthenticate admin (never registered, suffix must not act as a comment) -> NO_SUCH_USER\n\nstored rows (note: no password appears below):\n  alice  ->  pbkdf2-sha256$1000$LMyzicMWQjBHbUhDk4cb7Q==$ya8biQwDyomOHfTluePSOAwLNjT3rnrrfzmlycbyEUE=\n  bob  ->  pbkdf2-sha256$1000$rXRQfahOq1rNySC3awDFTg==$tKwZZpP3q1/CgsIBZ2f+ejVr7macDLAwTzJrS8ohX70=\n  admin--  ->  pbkdf2-sha256$1000$6UBnVV5IM0O3QIrb1meIJw==$/MNgVr1Df4AZnCJ4l/ugxtjK1lgDC8J9nXIbXyo/hME=\n```\n\nSalts are drawn fresh on every run, so your digests will differ from these even\nthough the passwords are the same -- that is the salt doing its job.\n\nThe demo builds its hasher with 1,000 iterations so it finishes immediately; a\nproduction `new PasswordHasher()` gets the 600,000-iteration default. Note that\n`alice` and `bob` received different salts, and that `admin--` is stored as a\nliteral username -- the trailing `--` does not act as a SQL comment because it\nnever enters the statement text.\n\n### Run the verification suites\n\nTwo suites, deliberately complementary:\n\n```bash\njavac -Xlint:all -Werror -d out \\\n    UserRegistration.java UserRegistrationH2Test.java UserRegistrationSqlContractTest.java\n\n# 1. SQL contract suite: no database, JDBC doubles via java.lang.reflect.Proxy.\njava -cp out UserRegistrationSqlContractTest\n\n# 2. Integration suite against a real engine.\njava -cp out:h2.jar UserRegistrationH2Test\n```\n\nCurrent results: **125 checks passed** and **86 checks passed** respectively\n(exit code 0; on failure each prints every failed check and exits 1).\n\n* `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest` records the exact statement text and every\n  bound parameter, so it can assert things no database would tell you: that a\n  credential appears nowhere in the statement text, that all three placeholders\n  were bound, that the data path never uses a plain `Statement`, that a\n  duplicate-key `SQLException` (state `23505`) becomes a conflict rather than a\n  retry, that a `22001` \"value too long\" is *not* mistaken for a conflict even\n  when the username happens to exist, that every statement is closed, and that\n  the unknown-username path really does pay for a derivation (~180-220 ms\n  against a doubled connection, where everything else is free). It also scripts\n  results a real engine will not hand you, such as a constraint violation on\n  insert.\n* `UserRegistrationH2Test` runs the same calls against H2, which is what catches\n  a predicate that now matches *too much*: under `username LIKE ?` a supplied\n  `alice_1` matches the row of an attacker-owned `alicex1`, and the real engine\n  is the only thing that can show that. It also covers transaction visibility\n  from a second connection, duplicate handling on real rows, a known-answer test\n  for the KDF, and the enumeration-timing equalisation as a best-of-5 ratio so\n  scheduler noise cannot decide the outcome.\n\nThe two suites are complementary, not redundant: several mutants were caught by\none suite and passed by the other.\n\n### Mutation results\n\nEvery weakening below was applied to the implementation on its own (22 mutants)\nand the suites re-run. **All 22 were caught**, each by a check that names the\nbehaviour. A surviving mutant would have meant the tests did not really cover\nthat behaviour, which is the only way to know a security test is a test.\n\n| Mutant | Caught by |\n| --- | --- |\n| Store the password in the clear | digest-format and no-password-in-row checks (both suites) |\n| `=` -> `LIKE` in the username lookup | H2: borrowed-row test; contract: statement-text test |\n| `UPPER(username) = UPPER(?)` | H2: case-sensitivity tests; contract: binding test |\n| Lookup no longer keyed on the supplied username | both suites fail end to end |\n| `==` instead of `MessageDigest.isEqual` | 14 checks |\n| Derive a 128-bit key instead of 256-bit | known-answer test (+14 checks) |\n| Drop the unknown-user timing guard | both timing checks (0 ms vs ~57 ms) |\n| Refuse a bad candidate for free | refused-candidate timing ratio check |\n| Unknown username authenticates | 7 checks |\n| Treat any constraint violation as a duplicate | `22001`-with-existing-row check |\n| Forget the commit | second-connection visibility + commit checks |\n| Remove the stored iteration bounds | bounded probe reports \"still deriving after 5000 ms\" |\n| Drop username validation | 9 checks |\n| Drop the password length cap | length checks |\n| Reuse one salt for every account | 3 checks |\n| Verify with the current default cost, not the stored one | migration test |\n| Stamp the wrong iteration count into the digest | contract timing check |\n| Lax (MIME) Base64 for a stored digest | smuggled-newline digest checks |\n| `clear()` stops zeroing | zeroing check |\n| Drop the ambiguous-password guard entirely | 14 checks |\n| Catch only the surrogate class | 7 checks |\n| Catch only the trailing-NUL class | 7 checks |\n\nTwo notes on method, because each decided a verdict:\n\n* The malformed-digest checks run each verification on a worker thread with a\n  5-second budget. Without that, deleting the iteration ceiling makes the suite\n  hang forever on a 999,999,999-round digest -- which is the denial of service\n  itself, but not a *reported* failure.\n* The timing checks compare best-of-N runs against each other as a ratio rather\n  than asserting a wall-clock number, so a loaded machine cannot make them\n  flake, and a guard that silently stopped deriving cannot pass a boolean-only\n  assertion.\n\n\n---\n\n## What this deliberately does not include\n\nThe task scoped a connection and credential storage. These are still required\nbefore this is a login system, and none of them belong in this file:\n\n* **Transport security.** Digest strength is irrelevant if the password crosses\n  the network in cleartext: TLS is the application's job.\n* **Rate limiting, lockout, and breach detection.** Nothing here throttles\n  repeated guesses against one account.\n* **Password policy and strength checking.** Only emptiness and length are\n  enforced; a minimum length, a common-password blocklist and a breach check\n  (`haveibeenpwned` k-anonymity API) are a product decision.\n* **A migration tool.** `ensureSchema()` is a convenience; run the DDL through\n  Flyway/Liquibase in anything real.\n* **Alternative KDFs.** Argon2id and scrypt are stronger choices when a library\n  is available, since they also resist GPU and ASIC cracking. This file sticks to\n  PBKDF2 because it is in every JDK and needs no third-party dependency; the\n  stored format already carries an algorithm tag, so a second algorithm can be\n  added to `PasswordHasher` without touching existing rows.\n* **Session issuance.** `authenticate` answers a question; it does not create a\n  session, cookie or token.\n* **Charset normalisation of usernames.** The allowlist keeps this moot\n  (ASCII-only). If you widen it to Unicode, normalise (NFKC) before storing or\n  homoglyph accounts become possible.\n\n## Files\n\n| File | Purpose |\n| --- | --- |\n| `UserRegistration.java` | The implementation: `PasswordHasher`, `UserRegistry`, and a `main` demonstration. |\n| `UserRegistrationH2Test.java` | Integration checks against a real H2 database. |\n| `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest.java` | SQL/parameter contract checks using JDBC doubles; needs no database. |\n| `README.md` | This document. |\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"ff4b6941-3977-476a-b76b-9014d6009a36","session_id":"8abe6239-b961-4eaf-a88d-781fc0f90061","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"ff4b6941-3977-476a-b76b-9014d6009a36","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"The README makes strong claims about security. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, H2's 23502 NULL-not-violated, 23503 referential, 23513 check violation), then `register` (line 503-509) reports `USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` whenever a row with that username happens to exist. So a genuine data fault (e.g. `created_at` null, a check constraint, an FK) is silently converted into a successful-looking \"already exists\" outcome, and the insert's real cause is never surfaced → the account is NOT created but the caller believes it's a benign duplicate. Real finding, Low/Medium: CWE-754 (Improper Check for Unusual or Exceptional Conditions) / CWE-703. Note the contract test specifically tests 22001-not-a-conflict, so the authors cared — but only about 22xxx.\n\nAlso confirmed `commitIfManaging` at 613-617: `connection.commit()` on the caller's connection.\n\nNow I have everything. Let me now write the report. Final finding list, severity-ordered:\n\n**SEC-01 — High — CWE-916**: `MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000` doubles as the acceptance floor for *stored* digests, and the public `PasswordHasher(int)` constructor plus `main()` mint 1,000-round digests. A stolen table whose rows were stamped at 1,000 rounds cracks ~600× faster than the README's advertised 600,000; `authenticate` never re-hashes at the current cost, so those rows live forever. Evidence: 114, 150-155, 351-352, 651; README demo output shows `pbkdf2-sha256$1000$...`; H2 migration test asserts 1,000-round rows keep verifying (line 668-686).\nFix: separate the floors — `MIN_ACCEPTED_STORED_ITERATIONS` (e.g. 600,000 or at least 310,000) vs a constructor floor, and re-hash on successful login when `parsed.iterations < iterations`.\n\n**SEC-02 — Medium — CWE-400**: The guard digest is derived in the `UserRegistry` constructor (line 447), so *every* registry construction costs a full PBKDF2 run at the hasher's cost (≈860 ms at the 600,000 default on this machine, per README). README prescribes \"one registry per request\", so an unauthenticated attacker costs themselves one cheap request and buys the server ~0.86 s of CPU, on every login request whether or not it authenticates. Also doubles the latency of legitimate requests.\nFix: derive the guard digest once per hasher (memoised on `PasswordHasher`), since only the iteration count needs to match; salt randomness is irrelevant for a digest that is never compared against a real user's input.\n\n**SEC-03 — Medium — CWE-664**: `register`/`ensureSchema` commit the caller's connection (613-617 called from 515 and 471). With `commitOwnWork=true` (default) and manual commits, any *unrelated* pending work on that connection is committed as a side effect of one account registration. \nFix: default `commitOwnWork=false`, or document + require the caller to pass a dedicated connection; better: only commit if this registry opened the transaction (e.g. track `connection.getAutoCommit()` and use savepoints), or leave commit to the caller. Simplest defensible fix in-code: require the caller to opt in explicitly (`new UserRegistry(conn, hasher, true)`), i.e. flip the 2-arg default to false. Careful — flipping the default is behaviour change; the fix block can show savepoint-based commit which is precise. I'll show savepoint-based as the recommended fix.\n\n**SEC-04 — Low — CWE-754**: `isUniqueViolation` accepts the entire 23xxx class; combined with the `exists(username)` confirmation, a genuine integrity fault on an existing username is reported as `USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` and the insert failure is swallowed.\nFix: match duplicate-key codes explicitly (H2/Postgres 23505, MySQL/MariaDB 23000 + vendor code 1062, Oracle 1) and fall back to `exists()` only for those.\n\n**SEC-05 — Low — CWE-204**: distinct `NO_SUCH_USER`/`BAD_PASSWORD` (533-566) re-open the enumeration oracle the guard exists to close, because the enum is public API and any caller that renders it differently (or logs it into a client-visible response) undoes the equalisation; `register` already leaks existence too (`USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` vs `CREATED`) — which is unavoidable for signup but must not be reachable pre-auth at volume.\nHmm — is the distinct enum a *finding* or a documented design decision? Executive Summary\n\n`UserRegistration.java` is a small, unusually careful JDBC component: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 password storage with per-account salts, self-describing digests, strictly parameterised SQL, an allowlist username validator, and a guard-digest timing equaliser. Almost every claim in `README.md` holds up under inspection, and the two dependency-free test suites corroborate the SQL and KDF contracts.\n\nSeven findings remain, one of which is material:\n\n- **SEC-01 (High)** is the reason to read this report. `MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000` does double duty as the *construction* floor **and** the floor accepted for a **stored** digest (`UserRegistration.java:113-114`, `351-353`). A 1,000-round digest is therefore permanently valid, and because `authenticate()` never re-derives and re-stores a cheaper row, an attacker who can write one row (or anyone who copies the shipped demo, which does exactly this at `UserRegistration.java:651`) gets a ~600x weaker password store that never self-heals. The shipped `README.md` demo output publishes digests of precisely this shape.\n- **SEC-02 (Medium)**: the component returns `NO_SUCH_USER` and `BAD_PASSWORD` as distinct values. The guard digest equalises the *timing* of the two paths but not the *value*, so every caller is one `if` away from re-opening username enumeration, and nothing in the API makes that mistake hard.\n- **SEC-03 - SEC-07 (Medium/Low)** cover first-row authentication when the unique index is absent, a too-broad `23xxx` integrity-state match that can launder a real data fault into a friendly \"username taken\", transaction-ownership overreach onto a caller-owned connection, the demo's password-in-argv plus digest-printing, and one test assertion that cannot fail.\n\nNo SQL injection, no weak randomness, no credential leak through logs or errors, and no algorithm-confusion path were found; those checks are itemised in section 6 rather than omitted, because in this component they are the controls the whole design leans on.\n\nVerified by execution during this review: `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest` passes **86/86** checks against the shipped `out/` classes; the shipped `.class` files are byte-identical to a fresh compile of the current source (so the artifacts are not stale and contain no extra SQL). The H2 suite could not be run — `h2.jar` is not present in the folder.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `UserRegistration.java` (727 lines) | The component: `PasswordHasher` (PBKDF2 storage), `UserRegistry` (register/authenticate over a caller-owned JDBC `Connection`), plus a `main` demo | KDF algorithm and iteration bounds; salt generation; digest parsing and comparison; username allowlist; all SQL text and parameter binding; uniqueness handling via constraint violation; commit/rollback ownership of a foreign transaction; demo CLI argument and stdout handling |\n| `UserRegistrationH2Test.java` (904 lines) | Integration suite against in-memory H2 | Asserts digest round-trip, refused ambiguous passwords, cost migration, unknown-user and refused-candidate timing ratios; writes SQL of its own (`DELETE`, `SELECT COUNT(*)`, injected-looking fixtures) |\n| `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest.java` (694 lines) | SQL/parameter contract suite using JDBC proxies; no database needed | Records and asserts exact statement text, that no password or quote character ever reaches statement text, that every placeholder is bound, that 23505 and 22001 are distinguished, and commit/rollback call counts |\n| `README.md` | Design rationale, API, claimed guarantees, demo transcript, mutation-testing table | Publishes `pbkdf2-sha256$1000$...` digests as sample output; documents stored-cost bounds of 1,000-10,000,000 as a control; instructs callers to render the two failure outcomes identically |\n| `out/*.class` (14 artifacts) | Committed build output | Would be the real deployed code if shipped; verified here against source (see section 6) |\n| `prompt.txt`, `prompt_hash.txt`, `cyber_prompt.txt`, `result.md`, `return.txt` | Task-harness metadata, not application code | Out of scope |\n\n## 3. Skills Applied\n\nPer the router skill's workflow, the skill-library names were grepped first (819 skills), the candidates' frontmatter descriptions skimmed, and only the relevant bodies opened and applied against this code. Each skill's own helper scripts were **not** executed: they are Python, and the router explicitly warns that running them against a Java target would produce spurious errors.\n\n| Skill | What it was applied to |\n| --- | --- |\n| `review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities` | The workflow itself: read every source file first, enumerate security-relevant behaviours, match them to library skills, apply each checklist with concrete `file:line`, report severity + CWE + fix |\n| `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application` | The on-point checklist (KDF parameter strength, hardcoded secrets, RNG usage). Its \"Weak KDF — low iteration PBKDF2\" row is what SEC-01 lands on; its \"Hardcoded Secrets\" row is what forced an explicit verdict on `GUARD_PASSWORD` (section 6) rather than a reflexive finding |\n| `testing-api-authentication-weaknesses` | The authentication-weakness taxonomy. Only partially applicable: it targets HTTP/JWT/session surfaces, and this component has no transport or session layer. Its \"weak password policies\" branch maps to the absent strength policy (section 6, informational); it does not cover password-storage cost, so SEC-01 does not rest on it |\n| `exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities` | The injection-point taxonomy (error-, boolean-, time- and OOB-based; second-order/stored execution) used as the checklist the code was tested against for `INSERT_SQL`, `SELECT_DIGEST_SQL`, `EXISTS_SQL`, the two DDL strings, and the demo's `'` and `--` payloads — no finding (section 6) |\n\nWhere no library skill covered a behaviour, that is stated at the finding. **SEC-01's severity rests on general knowledge** — OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet (600,000 iterations as the current PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 floor, credential-upgrade-on-login as the migration mechanism) and CWE-916 — as does **SEC-03**, **SEC-05** and **SEC-07**, which are transaction-semantics, API-design and test-assurance issues that the library's offensive checklists do not address. **SEC-02** was checked against `testing-api-authentication-weaknesses` and general CWE-204 knowledge; **SEC-04** against general CWE-754 knowledge; **SEC-06** against the hardcoded/argv-secret patterns of `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application`.\n\n## 4. Summary of Findings\n\n| ID | Title | Severity | CWE | Location |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| SEC-01 | 1,000-round digests are accepted forever, with no upgrade path | High | CWE-916 Insufficiently Protected Credentials | `UserRegistration.java:113-114`, `150-155`, `351-353`, `651` |\n| SEC-02 | Distinct `NO_SUCH_USER` / `BAD_PASSWORD` values re-open username enumeration | Medium | CWE-204 Observable Response Discrepancy | `UserRegistration.java:85-92`, `546-567` |\n| SEC-03 | Authentication trusts the first row of an unbounded `SELECT`; duplicates are silently ignored | Medium | CWE-367 Race Condition / Unverifiable Identity Binding | `UserRegistration.java:397-398`, `539-543` |\n| SEC-04 | Any `23xxx` integrity violation can be reported as \"username already exists\" | Low | CWE-754 Improper Check for Unusual or Exceptional Conditions | `UserRegistration.java:503-512`, `603-612` |\n| SEC-05 | `commitIfManaging()` commits the caller's unrelated pending work by default | Low | CWE-664 Improper Control of a Resource Through its Lifetime | `UserRegistration.java:428-429`, `614-618`, `620-632` |\n| SEC-06 | Demo takes the database password in argv and prints every stored digest | Low | CWE-214 Invocation of Privileged or Sensitive Code with Untrusted Input (argv exposure) | `UserRegistration.java:644-653`, `712-718` |\n| SEC-07 | Assertion that cannot fail hides a regression in the refused-candidate guard | Low | CWE-1023 Redundant Comparisons | `UserRegistrationH2Test.java:116-122` |\n\n## 5. Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01 — 1,000-round digests are accepted forever, with no upgrade path\n\n**Severity:** High\n\n**CWE:** CWE-916 Insufficiently Protected Credentials (weak KDF cost; CWE-326 inadequate key-strength analogue)\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistration.java:113-114` (the constant), `150-155` (constructor floor), `351-353` (stored-digest floor), `651` (demo uses it)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 600_000;\n\n    /** Floor accepted for a stored digest, so a hostile row cannot ask for 1 round. */\n    public static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n```\n\n```java\n        public PasswordHasher(int iterations) {\n            if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                        \"iterations must be in [\" + MIN_ITERATIONS + \", \" + MAX_ITERATIONS + \"]\");\n            }\n            this.iterations = iterations;\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n                if (iterationCount < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterationCount > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iteration count out of range: \" + iterationCount);\n                }\n```\n\n```java\n        // 1,000 rounds instead of 600,000 so the demo finishes at once. A\n        // production registry is built with no argument and gets the default.\n        PasswordHasher hasher = new PasswordHasher(PasswordHasher.MIN_ITERATIONS);\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The upper bound is a genuine DoS control and should stay. The lower bound is not: `MIN_ITERATIONS` is used for two different jobs, and the second one is dangerous. `Stored.parse` accepts *any* stored digest at or above 1,000 rounds, so 1,000 rounds is a permanently sanctioned cost for a live credential — the javadoc says so in as many words (\"Floor accepted for a stored digest\"). `authenticate()` verifies with whatever count the row carries and has no re-hash-and-`UPDATE` path (the only `executeUpdate()` in the file is the insert at line 501), so a cheap row never gets expensive. The result is a stable ~600x reduction in offline-cracking cost — 1,000 rounds of PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 is on the order of 1.4 ms per guess on the machine described in `README.md`, versus the 860 ms the design is built around — that survives every future hardening of the default. Three independent routes put such a row in place: an attacker with row-write on the table (a SQL-injection bug *anywhere else* in the application, a restore of an old backup, an unpatched write path) simply lowers the count on a target account and waits; `main()` creates exactly these rows and `README.md` prints them as sample output, so copy-pasting the documented demo or its output shape into a fixture, seed script, or support tool ships 1,000-round credentials; and any operator who constructs `new PasswordHasher(1_000)` \"for testing\" in a service that also serves production traffic gets no warning at all. `README.md` lists \"Stored-cost bounds (1,000 - 10,000,000)\" in its guarantees table, which frames the weak end of that range as a property rather than the hole.\n\n**Fix:** Split the two constants — keep the low value as the *construction* floor for tests and key-derivation call sites, and make the *stored* floor the OWASP-relevant cost — then add the upgrade path the design is already shaped for, so rows created earlier or tampered with are repaired the next time the user proves the password:\n\n```java\n        public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 600_000;\n\n        /** Lowest count a caller may ask a hasher to stamp. Tests live here. */\n        public static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n\n        /**\n         * Lowest count accepted in a stored digest. Deliberately far above\n         * MIN_ITERATIONS: a row below this is treated as corrupt or hostile,\n         * not as a cheap-but-legal account.\n         */\n        public static final int MIN_ACCEPTED_STORED_ITERATIONS = 600_000;\n\n        public PasswordHasher(int iterations) {\n            if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                        \"iterations must be in [\" + MIN_ITERATIONS + \", \" + MAX_ITERATIONS + \"]\");\n            }\n            this.iterations = iterations;\n        }\n\n        /** @return true when a stored row is cheaper than the current default and should be re-stamped */\n        public boolean wantsUpgrade(Stored stored) {\n            return stored.iterations < DEFAULT_ITERATIONS;\n        }\n\n        /** @return a digest of the same secret at this hasher's current cost */\n        public String rehash(char[] password, Stored stored) {\n            return hash(password);\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n                if (iterationCount < MIN_ACCEPTED_STORED_ITERATIONS\n                        || iterationCount > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                            \"iteration count out of range: \" + iterationCount);\n                }\n```\n\n```java\n            boolean authenticated = hasher.matches(password, storedDigest);\n            if (authenticated && hasher.wantsUpgrade(hasher.parseStored(storedDigest))) {\n                try (PreparedStatement upgrade = connection.prepareStatement(UPGRADE_DIGEST_SQL)) {\n                    upgrade.setString(1, hasher.hash(password));\n                    upgrade.setString(2, username);\n                    upgrade.executeUpdate();\n                }\n                commitIfManaging();\n            }\n            return authenticated\n                    ? AuthenticationOutcome.AUTHENTICATED\n                    : AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_PASSWORD;\n```\n\nwith `UPGRADE_DIGEST_SQL = \"UPDATE \" + TABLE + \" SET password_hash = ? WHERE username = ?\"`. If a migration window genuinely requires accepting older, cheaper rows, set `MIN_ACCEPTED_STORED_ITERATIONS` to the *known* historical cost (never to the test floor), record it as a tracked exception, and let the upgrade loop above drain the backlog — that is the whole point of storing the count per row. Independently, stop the demo from shipping the weak shape: `main()` should use `new PasswordHasher()` and accept a slower transcript.\n\n### SEC-02 — Distinct `NO_SUCH_USER` / `BAD_PASSWORD` values re-open username enumeration\n\n**Severity:** Medium\n\n**CWE:** CWE-204 Observable Response Discrepancy\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistration.java:85-92` (enum), `546-567` (both failure returns)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n    public enum AuthenticationOutcome {\n        /** The candidate password produced the stored digest. */\n        AUTHENTICATED,\n        /** No row owns that username. */\n        NO_SUCH_USER,\n        /** A row exists and the candidate password did not produce its digest. */\n        BAD_PASSWORD\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n            if (!PasswordHasher.isVerifiableCandidate(password)) {\n                // A blank, over-long or JCE-ambiguous candidate can never match,\n                // but it must not be answered cheaply either: a fast refusal\n                // would let anyone tell \"user exists\" from \"user absent\" by\n                // timing, which is the whole leak the guard digest exists to\n                // close. But it buys equal *cost*, and then the method hands the caller a value that is not equal: `NO_SUCH_USER` versus `BAD_PASSWORD`. The entire defence against username enumeration is therefore a prose instruction — `README.md` \"Show the caller one identical message\", and the javadoc's \"Render `NO_SUCH_USER` and `BAD_PASSWORD` identically\" — with no structure enforcing it. A login handler that writes `return outcome == NO_SUCH_USER ? \"no such account\" : \"wrong password\"`, one that returns 404 versus 401, one that logs the two at different severities into a user-visible audit pane, or a support tool exposed behind a low-privilege role, all rebuild the account-enumeration oracle in a single line. That oracle is the precondition for credential stuffing against a known-real account list and for targeted phishing that names actual employees, and it is the exact weakness this component otherwise spends a full extra PBKDF2 run per failed attempt to close. Note the asymmetry: the value leaks instantly and reliably on every failed login, whereas the timing channel the guard was built for is noisy and rate-limited.\n\n**Fix:** Keep the distinction available for server-side telemetry, but make the un-collapsible-by-accident rendering the default surface, and stop returning the discriminator on the value every caller has to handle:\n\n```java\n    public enum AuthenticationOutcome {\n        /** The candidate password produced the stored digest. */\n        AUTHENTICATED,\n        /** Either no row owns that username or the secret was wrong: the two are\n         *  deliberately not distinguishable to the caller. */\n        BAD_CREDENTIALS;\n\n        public boolean isAuthenticated() {\n            return this == AUTHENTICATED;\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n            if (!PasswordHasher.isVerifiableCandidate(password)) {\n                hasher.matches(GUARD_PASSWORD, guardDigest);\n                LOGGER.log(Level.INFO, \"login refused candidate user={0} reason={1}\",\n                        new Object[] {username, found ? \"candidate\" : \"no-row\"});\n                return AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_CREDENTIALS;\n            }\n\n            if (!found) {\n                hasher.matches(GUARD_PASSWORD, guardDigest);\n                LOGGER.log(Level.INFO, \"login for absent user {0}\", username);\n                return AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_CREDENTIALS;\n            }\n            boolean ok = hasher.matches(password, storedDigest);\n            if (!ok) {\n                LOGGER.log(Level.INFO, \"login bad password for {0}\", username);\n            }\n            return ok ? IF NOT EXISTS`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n        private static final String SELECT_DIGEST_SQL =\n                \"SELECT password_hash FROM \" + TABLE + \" WHERE username = ?\";\n```\n\n```java\n            String storedDigest = null;\n            boolean found = false;\n            try (PreparedStatement select = connection.prepareStatement(SELECT_DIGEST_SQL)) {\n                select.setString(1, username);\n                try (ResultSet rows = select.executeQuery()) {\n                    if (rows.next()) {\n                        found = true;\n                        storedDigest = rows.getString(1);\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `authenticate()` derives the account's verifier from whichever row the engine happens to return first, and quietly ignores any others. The design assumes that is safe because `ensureSchema()` creates a unique index, but three ordinary situations break that assumption, and none of them is visible from inside this class. `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` is a no-op if the index name already exists with different contents, and on most engines it *fails outright* rather than repairing a pre-existing table that already holds duplicate usernames — so a database that predates this component, or one where a DBA renamed or dropped `ux_app_users_username`, has no uniqueness guarantee at all while `register()` keeps returning `CREATED`. A restore-from-backup plus a re-seed produces two `alice` rows with different hashes; the same happens if the schema is ever applied to a table where uniqueness was expressed as a non-unique index for query performance. From then on, login for `alice` silently authenticates against row order: the moment the planner switches (a new index, `ORDER BY` change, vacuum, engine upgrade, or a concurrent insert moving the physical order), the account starts rejecting its real owner while the older digest stays valid for anyone who knows it. That is an identity-resolution failure, not a cosmetic bug — it authenticates a principal against an unverified credential record, and because the code never counts rows, there is no error, no log, and no way to notice. The uniqueness check in `register()` cannot protect login here either: it only inspects the error path of an `INSERT` that a duplicate on a missing index lets through.\n\n**Fix:** Make the ambiguity explicit and fail closed — fetch up to two rows and refuse to guess — and stop relying on `IF NOT EXISTS` to have established the constraint:\n\n```java\n        private static final String SELECT_DIGEST_SQL =\n                \"SELECT password_hash FROM \" + TABLE + \" WHERE username = ? FETCH FIRST 2 ROWS ONLY\";\n```\n\n```java\n            String storedDigest = null;\n            boolean found = false;\n            int candidates = 0;\n            try (PreparedStatement select = connection.prepareStatement(SELECT_DIGEST_SQL)) {\n                select.setString(1, username);\n                try (ResultSet rows = select.executeQuery()) {\n                    while (rows.next()) {\n                        candidates++;\n                        if (!found) {\n                            found = true;\n                            storedDigest = rows.getString(1);\n                        }\n                    }\n                }\n            }\n            if (candidates > 1) {\n                // Two rows claim this username: the verifier is ambiguous, so no\n                // login can be honoured until the data is repaired. Fail closed.\n                hasher.matches(GUARD_PASSWORD, guardDigest);\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\"ambiguous account for \" + describe(username)\n                        + \": \" + candidates + \" rows share a username, no unique index?\");\n            }\n```\n\n`FETCH FIRST 2 ROWS ONLY` is the portable spelling (H2, PostgreSQL, and since 1.7 MariaDB/MySQL 8); `LIMIT 2` also works on those engines. Pair it with a startup assertion in the embedding service — `SELECT username, COUNT(*) ... GROUP BY username HAVING COUNT(*) > 1` must return nothing, and `SHOW INDEX`/`pg_indexes` must show the unique constraint — because `ensureSchema()` cannot be trusted to have created it.\n\n### SEC-04 — Any `23xxx` integrity violation can be reported as \"username already exists\"\n\n**Severity:** Low\n\n**CWE:** CWE-754 Improper Check for Unusual or Exceptional Conditions (improper cause discrimination)\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistration.java:503-512` (`register` catch), `603-612` (`isUniqueViolation`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n            } catch (SQLException rejected) {\n                if (isUniqueViolation(rejected)) {\n                    // The index is the authority on uniqueness, so confirm the\n                    // loser of the race is really the same username before\n                    // reporting a friendly conflict.\n                    if (exists(username)) {\n                        rollbackIfManaging();\n                        return RegistrationOutcome.USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS;\n                    }\n                }\n                rollbackIfManaging();\n                throw rejected;\n            }\n```\n\n```java\n        /** SQL states for constraint violations: 23xxx in H2, PostgreSQL, MariaDB. */\n        private static boolean isUniqueViolation(SQLException failure) {\n            for (SQLException current = failure; current != null; current = current.getNextException()) {\n                String state = current.getSQLState();\n                if (state != null && state.startsWith(\"23\")) {\n                    return true;\n                }\n            }\n            return false;\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `23xxx` is the whole *integrity constraint violation* class, not uniqueness. It covers NOT NULL (`23502` in H2, `23502`/`407` variants elsewhere), check violations (`23513`, `4025`), referential integrity (`23503`, `1451`/`1452`), and exclusion violations — as well as unique/primary-key (`23505`, `23000`, `23001`, MySQL `23000`/`1062`). The code's own comment states the intent — \"confirm the loser of the race is really the same username before reporting a friendly conflict\" — but `exists(username)` does not confirm *cause*, only *coincidence*: it re-asks whether the username is present, which is true for every retry against an existing account regardless of why the insert failed. So the sequence \"someone re-registers `alice`, and the insert fails because `created_at` was NOT NULL-nullable, the `VARCHAR(200)` digest column overflowed an unusual cost stamp, a trigger raises `23514`, or a later FK to a `tenants` table is un满足\" resolves to a clean `USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` with the real `SQLException` discarded and no log. Registration then reports a benign, permanent-sounding condition while the actual fault is a broken schema or data path that will keep failing, and the operator's only clue is \"username taken\" for an account they already know exists. The failure is *silent in the direction that matters*: a genuine outage presents as expected user behaviour. The contract suite narrows the blast radius but does not close it — it proves a `22001` (data-truncation class) is rethrown, which is a different class entirely, so nothing in the suite exercises `23502`/`23503`/`23513` at all.\n\n**Fix:** Match duplicate-key states and vendor codes explicitly, and require the confirming `exists()` to agree with the specific constraint that fired:\n\n```java\n PostgreSQL / MariaDB / SQL Server class used for unique violations\n                \"23001\", // SQL Server restrictive\n                \"23505\", // H2, PostgreSQL: unique constraint\n                \"23503\", \"23502\", \"23513\", \"23514\"); // NOT NULL / FK / check: never duplicates\n\n        private static final Set<Integer> DUPLICATE_VENDOR_CODES = Set.of(\n                1062,   // MySQL / MariaDB ER_DUP_ENTRY\n                2601,   // SQL Server unique index\n                2627,   // SQL Server primary key\n                1);     // Oracle ORA-00001\n\n        private static boolean isDuplicateKey(SQLException failure) {\n            for (SQLException current = failure; current != null; current = current.getNextException()) {\n                String state = current.getSQLState();\n                int code = current.getErrorCode();\n                if (state != null && DUPLICATE_SQL_STATES.contains(state)\n                        && !DUPLICATE_SQL_STATES.contains(\"23505\") == false || codeMatches(code)) {\n                    ...\n                }\n            }\n            return false;\n        }\n```\n\nConcretely, and simpler — treat only these as a conflict and rethrow everything else after logging:\n\n```java\n        private static boolean isDuplicateKey(SQLException failure) {\n            for (SQLException current = failure; current != null; current = current.getNextException()) {\n                String state = current.getSQLState();\n                int code = current.getErrorCode();\n                boolean stateSaysDuplicate = \"23000\".equals(state)\n                        || \"23001\".equals(state) || \"23505\".equals(state);\n                boolean codeSaysDuplicate = code == 1062 || code == 2601 || code == 2627 || code == 1;\n                if (stateSaysDuplicate || codeSaysDuplicate) {\n                    return true;\n                }\n            }\n            return false;\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n            } catch (SQLException rejected) {\n                if (isDuplicateKey(rejected) && exists(username)) {\n                    rollbackIfManaging();\n exception is rethrown — today's suite covers only `22001`.\n\n### SEC-05 — `commitIfManaging()` commits the caller's unrelated pending work by default\n\n**Severity:** Low\n\n**CWE:** CWE-664 Improper Control of a Resource Through its Lifetime (transaction ownership)\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistration.java:428-429` (defaulting constructor), `516` / `471` (commit sites), `614-618`, `620-632`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n        public UserRegistry(Connection connection, PasswordHasher hasher) {\n            this(connection, hasher, true);\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n        private void commitIfManaging() throws SQLException {\n            if (commitOwnWork && !connection.getAutoCommit()) {\n                connection.commit();\n            }\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n        private void rollbackIfManaging() {\n            if (!commitOwnWork) {\n                return;\n            }\n            try {\n                if (!connection.getAutoCommit()) {\n                    connection.rollback();\n                }\n            } catch (SQLException couldNotRollBack) {\n                // The original failure is the one worth reporting; a failed\n                // rollback on a dying connection must not mask it.\n            }\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The class is scrupulous about not closing the caller's `Connection`, but it is willing to *commit* it, and the two-argument constructor — the one `README.md` shows in its usage example — turns that on by default. `commit()` and `rollback()` are connection-wide, not statement-scoped, so on a manual-commit connection one successful `register()` finalises every other pending statement in the same transaction: the account row plus, say, a half-built order, a partially applied entitlement change, or the rest of a batch the surrounding service still intended to validate and roll back. Worse, `register()`'s own conflict path calls `rollbackIfManaging()` *before* returning `USERNAME_ALREADY_EXISTS` — an outcome that is not an error — so a normal \"that name is taken\" response discards the caller's unrelated work as if the operation had failed, while the caller's own return value tells them nothing went wrong. The rollback failure is then swallowed outright, so if the rollback itself fails the caller proceeds inside a transaction that is still holding the earlier writes, with no signal at all. The escape hatch exists and is documented (`commitOwnWork = false`), which is why this is Low rather than High — but the safe setting is opt-in, the API's default and its documented example pick the unsafe one, and the damage lands on data the component was never given.\n\n**Fix:** Default to the caller owning the transaction, and scope the rollback to a savepoint so the component can undo only its own insert:\n\n```java\n        /**\n         * A registry that leaves commit decisions to the caller: the safe default\n         * when the connection may carry a larger unit of work.\n         */\n        public UserRegistry(Connection connection, PasswordHasher hasher) {\n            this(connection, hasher, false);\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n        private void rollbackOwnWork(Savepoint own) {\n            if (!commitOwnWork || own == null) {\n                return;\n            }\n            try {\n                connection.rollback(own);\n            } catch (SQLException couldNotRollBack) {\n                LOGGER.log(Level.WARNING, \"could not roll back registration savepoint\",\n                        couldNotRollBack);\n            }\n        }\n```\n\nwith `register()` taking the savepoint when it manages commits:\n\n```java\n            Savepoint own = null;\n            if (commitOwnWork && connection.getAutoCommit() == false) {\n                own = connection.setSavepoint(\"USER_REGISTRATION\");\n            }\n```\n\nand calling `commitIfManaging()` only on `CREATED`, `rollbackOwnWork(own)` on the duplicate path. If committing on the caller's behalf must remain the default for compatibility, the alternative that removes the hazard entirely is a precondition: reject a connection that already has pending work (`connection.getAutoCommit()` true, or require the caller to pass a freshly allocated connection), so the component can never be the thing that finalises someone else's transaction.\n\n### SEC-06 — Demo takes the database password in argv and prints every stored digest\n\n**Severity:** Low\n\n**CWE:** CWE-214 Invocation of Privileged or Sensitive Code with Untrusted Input (secrets in process arguments); CWE-532 for the digest dump\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistration.java:644-653` (argument handling, defaults), `712-718` (digest printing)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n    public static void main(String[] args) throws SQLException {\n        String url = args.length > 0 ? args[0] : \"jdbc:h2:mem:demo;DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1\";\n        String user = args.length > 1 ? args[1] : \"sa\";\n        String secret = args.length > 2 ? args[2] : \"\";\n```\n\n```java\n            println(\"stored rows (note: no password appears below):\");\n            try (Statement statement = connection.createStatement();\n                 ResultSet rows = statement.executeQuery(\n                         \"SELECT username, password_hash FROM \" + TABLE + \" ORDER BY id\")) {\n                while (rows.next()) {\n                    println(\"  \" + rows.getString(1) + \"  ->  \" + rows.getString(2));\n                }\n            }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `README.md` tells the reader to run `java -cp out:h2.jar UserRegistration <jdbc-url> <user> <password>`, which is a documented instruction to type a real database credential into a command line. That places it in shell history, in the process arguments readable by any local account through `ps`/`/proc/<pid>/cmdline`, in the logs of every wrapper that records the commands it runs (CI job output, `script`, auditd exec records, bash/Zsh history sync, IDE run configurations), and in the terminal scrollback — while the empty-string default and the `sa` default quietly encourage the same shape against a real URL by omitting the argument entirely rather than refusing. `main()` also prints every stored digest to stdout, under a caption that emphasises the passwords are absent. Digests are not passwords, but they are the entire offline-cracking input, and combined with SEC-01 the sample rows are `pbkdf2-sha256$1000$...` — trivially brutable. stdout is routinely captured: pasted into tickets, archived in CI artifacts, screenshotted into chat. The `README.md` transcript already circulates real-looking digests of that weak shape as reference material, so anyone reproducing the demo has an easy habit of pointing this program at \"just a small test database\" that turns out to hold production accounts, and dumping it.\n\n**Fix:** Never accept the secret in argv, refuse rather than default, and do not print verifiers:\n\n```java\n    public static void main(String[] args) throws SQLException {\n        String url = args.length > 0 ? args[0] : \"jdbc:h2:mem:demo;DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1\";\n        if (!url.startsWith(\"jdbc:h2:mem:\")) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"refusing a non-in-memory URL: this demo dumps its table to stdout\");\n        }\n        String user = args.length > 1 ? args[1] : \"sa\";\n\n        // Never args[3]: argv is visible in ps(1)/procfs and lands in shell history.\n        String secret = System.getenv(\"DEMO_DB_PASSWORD\");\n        if (secret == null) {\n            Console console = System.console();\n            if (console == null) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(\n                        \"set DEMO_DB_PASSWORD or run from a terminal; not read from argv\");\n            }\n            char[] typed = console.readPassword(\"database password: \");\n            secret = new String(typed);\n        }\n```\n\nand replace the row dump with a non-reversible summary:\n\n```java\n            println(\"stored rows (username and cost only):\");\n            try (Statement statement = connection.createStatement();\n                 ResultSet rows = statement.executeQuery(\n                         \"SELECT username, password_hash FROM \" + TABLE + \" ORDER BY id\")) {\n                while (rows.next()) {\n                    String hash = rows.getString(2);\n                    println(\"  \" + rows.getString(1) + \"  ->  \"\n                            + hash.split(\"\\\\$\")[0] + \" iterations=\" + hash.split(\"\\\\$\")[1]);\n                }\n            }\n```\n\nso the demonstration still shows the self-describing digest format without exporting crackable material. Update `README.md`'s run instructions to match, and have the demo's own hashers use `new PasswordHasher()` so no 1,000-round example is ever generated.\n\n### SEC-07 — Assertion that cannot fail hides a regression in the refused-candidate guard\n\n**Severity:** Low\n\n**CWE:** CWE-1023 Redundant Comparisons (an assertion whose branches are identical, hence unfalsifiable)\n\n**Location:** `UserRegistrationH2Test.java:116-122`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n        for (String wrong : wrongOnes) {\n            char[] candidate = wrong.toCharArray();\n            check(\"rejects password [\" + describe(wrong) + \"]\",\n                    registry.authenticate(\"authcase\", candidate)\n                            == (wrong.isEmpty()\n                            ? UserRegistration.AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_PASSWORD\n                            : UserRegistration.AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_PASSWORD));\n            UserRegistration.PasswordHasher.clear(candidate);\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The conditional yields `BAD_PASSWORD` on both sides, so the ternary is dead and the `wrong.isEmpty()` test decides nothing. The empty-candidate case is the interesting one: an empty password must be refused by the *candidate-validation* path (`isVerifiableCandidate`) rather than by an accidental mismatch deep inside PBKDF2, and it is the case most likely to be \"fixed\" into acceptance by a plausible-looking refactor — dropping the empty check as \"unnecessary\", or letting `PBEKeySpec` with a zero-length password derive a real digest, which turns `\"\"` into a valid credential for any account whose digest happens to match. If that happens, this line still prints `ok` because the empty candidate would still return `BAD_PASSWORD` for the wrong reason (or, worse, the assertion machinery would report the check as passing while it verifies no property). The defect is not only the vacuous branch: this whole loop asserts only the *outcome*, while `README.md`'s mutation table claims the guard is protected by the *timing* ratio check (\"Refuse a bad candidate for free | refused-candidate timing ratio check\") — so the boolean check here reads as coverage for the refused-candidate behaviour without actually pinning the mechanism, and the two together make the control look better tested than it is.\n\n**Fix:** Assert the property that is actually intended, per case, and make the empty case distinguishable from a plain wrong password:\n\n```java\n        for (String wrong : wrongOnes) {\n            char[] candidate = wrong.toCharArray();\n            UserRegistration.AuthenticationOutcome outcome =\n                    registry.authenticate(\"authcase\", candidate);\n            if (wrong.isEmpty()) {\n                // The empty candidate must be refused as an unusable candidate,\n                // and must not be answered more cheaply than a real guess: pin the\n                // outcome here and the cost in the timing suite.\n                check(\"empty candidate is refused as a bad credential\",\n                        outcome == UserRegistration.AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_PASSWORD);\n            } else {\n                check(\"rejects password [\" + describe(wrong) + \"]\",\n                        outcome == UserRegistration.AuthenticationOutcome.BAD_PASSWORD);\n            }\n            UserRegistration.PasswordHasher.clear(candidate);\n        }\n```\n\nIf SEC-02's two-valued outcome is adopted, keep the branches but compare against `BAD_CREDENTIALS`, and add a companion check that the empty candidate costs a full derivation (best-of-N ratio against a wrong password, as the timing suite already does for the ambiguous candidate at `UserRegistrationH2Test.java:783-798`) — that is the assertion the mutation table believes exists.\n\n## 6. Verified Controls and Checks With No Finding\n\nControls confirmed by reading, and where stated by execution or bytecode inspection:\n\n- **SQL injection — none.** Every statement carrying user data is a compile-time constant with `?` placeholders (`INSERT_SQL`, `SELECT_DIGEST_SQL`, `EXISTS_SQL`); the only concatenation is into the `TABLE` constant. The username never reaches statement text, and it is validated against `[A-Za-z0-9._-]{3,64}` *before* any statement is prepared, so `'`, `;`, `--` and whitespace never reach the parser. `WHERE username = ?` is used rather than `LIKE`, so no wildcard or escape semantics apply; matching is case-sensitive and verbatim, so the `admin--` and `admin' OR '1'='1` cases behave as literals (confirmed by the demo transcript and by `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest`, which asserts no quote character, no `--`, no `/*` ever appears in recorded statement text, that no password string appears there, and that every placeholder was bound). Checked for error-, boolean-, time- and OOB-based injection and for second-order execution: the stored username is only ever compared, never re-embedded in SQL. `UserRegistrationSqlContractTest` passes **86/86** against the shipped `out/` classes (run during this review).\n- **KDF algorithm confusion / downgrade — none.** `Stored.parse` requires `parts.length == 4` and `FORMAT_ID.equals(parts[0])`, so a row cannot claim `pbkdf2-sha1`, `md5`, `sha256` or a bare hex digest and have it honoured; the KDF name is a fixed `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256` literal, not read from the row. Combined with the salt/digest length checks (`SALT_BYTES = 16`, `DIGEST_BYTES = 32`) and strict `Base64.getDecoder()` (not the MIME variant), a hostile row cannot shrink the inputs. The upper bound of 10,000,000 rounds is a working login-DoS control (see SEC-01 for the lower bound).\n- **Credential comparison — sound.** `MessageDigest.isEqual` on two fixed-length 32-byte arrays, so no short-circuit timing gradient and no length oracle; salt is 128 bits from a per-call `SecureRandom`; `PBEKeySpec` and the scratch digest are zeroed afterwards; the caller's `char[]` is left intact with an explicit `clear()` helper.\n- **`GUARD_PASSWORD` is not a credential leak.** Reviewed explicitly against `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application`'s hardcoded-secrets check. It is a fixed literal, but its `matches()` result is discarded on both guard paths, the guard digest carries no account, is salted per registry instance, and never leaves the object; revealing the string would not authenticate anyone, because there is no row that verifies against it. Worth one sentence in the class javadoc so a future reader does not \"remediate\" it into a config value and lose the guard — but not a finding.\n- **Timing equalisation for the unknown-user path — present and effective** as a *timing* control (SEC-02 is about the value, not the clock): both the absent-row path and the refused-candidate path pay a full derivation, `README.md` measures ratios of 1.00-1.02 and 0.99-1.04, and `UserRegistrationH2Test` asserts best-of-N ratios with a 0.5 floor rather than absolute latencies, so the check is not machine-speed fragile.\n- **Password canonicalisation ambiguity — handled deliberately.** Ambiguous candidates (trailing NULs that `PBEKeySpec` drops, unpaired surrogates that encode to `?`) are refused as unverifiable rather than silently normalised, which is the right call and is tested (`UserRegistrationH2Test.java:466-534`), including a positive check that interior NULs and supplementary characters stay valid.\n- **Error-surface hygiene — good.** `describe()` reports `length N` only; the username never appears in exception messages; the swallowed `SQLException` in `rollbackIfManaging` is deliberate and commented so it cannot mask the real failure (its transactional consequence is covered in SEC-05).\n- **Resource handling — no leak found.** Every `PreparedStatement`, `Statement`, `ResultSet` and the demo `Connection` are in try-with-resources; the caller's `Connection` is never closed by the component (asserted by the contract suite). `ensureSchema()` runs two DDL statements through one `Statement` and commits once — no statement leak.\n- **Committed build artifacts are current — no supply-chain discrepancy.** All 14 `out/*.class` files are byte-identical (SHA-256) to a fresh `javac 17.0.20.1` compile of the present sources, e.g. `out/UserRegistration.class` = `c2ae6119b9811db0a7406974fcb6835c29f7dc69ab607a3e663f09ff7e067d14`. `javap -v` confirms the shipped constant pool holds exactly the five SQL strings from the source and no others, no `StringBuilder` anywhere in `UserRegistry` bytecode (so no runtime SQL concatenation), and that the compiled `Stored.parse` and `PasswordHasher(int)` really enforce `1000`/`10000000` (`sipush 1000` / `if_icmplt`). No credentials, URLs, reflection, `Runtime.exec`, or `Class.forName` appear in any shipped class. *Hygiene note, not a finding:* `out/` should not be version-controlled — nothing pins which compiler produced it, and every future source edit silently makes it stale even though today it is not.\n- **Test suites reviewed for their own hazards — clean.** Both test files touch only in-memory H2 (`jdbc:h2:mem:...`), no sockets, no `ProcessBuilder`/`Runtime.exec`, no file writes, no reflection; their injected-looking strings (`Robert'); DROP TABLE app_users;--`, `admin' OR '1'='1`) are fixtures and assertion text, and the `DELETE`/`SELECT COUNT(*)` statements they issue are test-only. The one assurance gap found in them is SEC-07.\n- **Not verified by execution.** `UserRegistrationH2Test` could not be run: no `h2.jar` exists anywhere in the folder, so the 125-check integration suite and every timing ratio in `README.md` are taken on the authors' word. `README.md`'s mutation-testing claims (22/22 mutants killed) were likewise not reproduced — no mutation tooling or build definition is present (no `pom.xml`, `build.gradle`, or `Makefile`), and the absence of version control means no build provenance is recorded.\n\nInformational gaps, disclaimed by `README.md` itself and therefore out of the component's stated scope rather than findings, but they must be closed by whatever embeds this class: no rate limiting or account lockout (so a 1,000-round row from SEC-01 is crackable online as well as offline), no password strength policy, no transport security, no session or token issuance, no NFKC normalisation, and `created_at` taken from wall-clock `LocalDateTime.now()` rather than the database clock. The absence of an Argon2id option is a documented design decision; PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 at 600,000 rounds is acceptable, and SEC-01 is about the cost actually stored, not the choice of primitive.\nBLOCK_304\nafter=$(wc -l < \"$F\")\necho \"lines before=$before after=$after added=$((after-before))\"\ngrep -c '^### SEC-' \"$F\"; grep -n '^## ' \"$F\"","description":"Append findings SEC-03..SEC-07 and section 6 to security_review.md, then verify structure"}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":154862,"output_tokens":7032,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":161894}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"f136128e-8cd1-418a-97c0-3d4936c83d25","session_id":"8abe6239-b961-4eaf-a88d-781fc0f90061","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_65cf00de66684cb282361728","is_error":false,"content":"lines before=237 after=621 added=384\n7\n3:## 1.
