The lower bound is not: `MIN_ITERATIONS` is used for two different jobs, and the second one is dangerous. `Stored.parse` accepts *any* stored digest at or above 1,000 rounds, so 1,000 rounds is a permanently sanctioned cost for a live credential — the javadoc says so in as many words (\"Floor accepted for a stored digest\"). `authenticate()` verifies with whatever count the row carries and has no re-hash-and-`UPDATE` path (the only `executeUpdate()` in the file is the insert at line 501), so a cheap row never gets expensive. The result is a stable ~600x reduction in offline-cracking cost — 1,000 rounds of PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 is on the order of 1.4 ms per guess on the machine described in `README.md`, versus the 860 ms the design is built around — that survives every future hardening of the default. Three independent routes put such a row in place: an attacker with row-write on the table (a SQL-injection bug *anywhere else* in the application, a restore of an old backup, an unpatched write path) simply lowers the count on a target account and waits; `main()` creates exactly these rows and `README.md` prints them as sample output, so copy-pasting the documented demo or its output shape into a fixture, seed script, or support tool ships 1,000-round credentials; and any operator who constructs `new PasswordHasher(1_000)` \"for testing\" in a service that also serves production traffic gets no warning at all. `README.md` lists \"Stored-cost bounds (1,000 - 10,000,000)\" in its guarantees table, which frames the weak end of that range as a property rather than the hole.\n96:**Fix:** Split the two constants — keep the low value as the *construction* floor for tests and key-derivation call sites, and make the *stored* floor the OWASP-relevant cost — then add the upgrade path the design is already shaped for, so rows created earlier or tampered with are repaired the next time the user proves the password:\n153:with `UPGRADE_DIGEST_SQL = \"UPDATE \" + TABLE + \" SET password_hash = ? WHERE username = ?\"`. If a migration window genuinely requires accepting older, cheaper rows, set `MIN_ACCEPTED_STORED_ITERATIONS` to the *known* historical cost (never to the test floor), record it as a tracked exception, and let the upgrade loop above drain the backlog — that is the whole point of storing the count per row. Independently, stop the demo from shipping the weak shape: `main()` should use `new PasswordHasher()` and accept a slower transcript.\n155:### SEC-02 — Distinct `NO_SUCH_USER` / `BAD_PASSWORD` values re-open username enumeration\n198:**Why it is a problem:** The guard digest is real work and it works — the measured unknown-user / wrong-password ratio is 1.00-1.02, and the refused-candidate path is equalised too. But it buys equal *cost*, and then the method hands the caller a value that is not equal: `NO_SUCH_USER` versus `BAD_PASSWORD`. The entire defence against username enumeration is therefore a prose instruction — `README.md` \"Show the caller one identical message\", and the javadoc's \"Render `NO_SUCH_USER` and `BAD_PASSWORD` identically\" — with no structure enforcing it. A login handler that writes `return outcome == NO_SUCH_USER ? \"no such account\" : \"wrong password\"`, one that returns 404 versus 401, one that logs the two at different severities into a user-visible audit pane, or a support tool exposed behind a low-privilege role, all rebuild the account-enumeration oracle in a single line. That oracle is the precondition for credential stuffing against a known-real account list and for targeted phishing that names actual employees, and it is the exact weakness this component otherwise spends a full extra PBKDF2 run per failed attempt to close. Note the asymmetry: the value leaks instantly and reliably on every failed login, whereas the timing channel the guard was built for is noisy and rate-limited.\n== table rows in section 4 ==\n7\n== total lines ==\n592"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"f431f926-6998-47cb-b849-3dbeb868b8ee","session_id":"8abe6239-b961-4eaf-a88d-781fc0f90061","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"f431f926-6998-47cb-b849-3dbeb868b8ee","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I'm realizing the grep pattern I used is matching closing fences that are legitimately bare ``` markers, not untagged opening fences. Showing lines 5-13 of 593 from the edited file:\n\n---\n\n`UserRegistration.java` is a small, unusually careful JDBC component: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 password storage with per-account salts, self-describing digests, strictly parameterised SQL, an allowlist username validator, and a guard-digest timing equaliser. Almost every claim in `README.md` holds up under inspection, and the two dependency-free test suites corroborate the SQL and KDF contracts.\n\nSeven findings remain, one of which is material:\n\n- **SEC-01 (High)** is the reason to read this report. `MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000` does double duty as the *construction* floor **and** the floor accepted for a **stored** digest (`UserRegistration.java:111-114`, `351-353`). A 1,000-round digest is therefore permanently valid, and because `authenticate()` never re-derives and re-stores a cheaper row, an attacker who can write one row (or anyone who copies the shipped demo, which does exactly this at `UserRegistration.java:651`) gets a ~600x weaker password store that never self-heals. The shipped `README.md` demo output publishes digests of precisely this shape.\n- **SEC-02 (Medium)**: the component returns `NO_SUCH_USER` and `BAD_PASSWORD` as distinct values. The guard digest equalises the *timing* of the two paths but not the *value*, so every caller is one `if` away from re-opening username enumeration, and nothing in the API makes that mistake hard.\n- **SEC-03 - SEC-07 (Medium/Low)** cover first-row authentication when the unique index is absent, a too-broad `23xxx` integrity-state match that can launder a real data fault into a friendly \"username taken\", transaction-ownership overreach onto a caller-owned connection, the demo's password-in-argv plus digest-printing, and one test assertion that cannot fail.\n\nNo SQL injection, no weak controls the whole design leans
